From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose it’s this trouble I’ve been having with bridges. I know that may sound a bit silly at my age, but when I realize I can’t avoid crossing one, my heart starts pounding right away. My hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear before I’m even on the bridge. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly bridges, yes. I don’t mean to make too much of it, but especially the high ones. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day without anything happening, but if I see one coming up on a map or through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just… waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across a bridge?\n\nPatient: It can happen either way, really. I don’t have to be the one driving. Once it’s clear we can’t avoid crossing the bridge, I start reacting before we’re even on it—my heart pounds, my hands get damp on the wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear. I know it sounds a bit much, but that’s how it comes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience fear or anxiety in other enclosed or high places, such as tunnels or tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor. At least, I haven’t noticed the same sort of thing with tunnels or tall buildings. It’s really the bridges that seem to set me off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges less intense when you cross a low bridge, such as one over a small stream?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is less intense if it’s just a low little bridge. It’s really those high spans that get to me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but when I see one coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. I realize that’s not very logical.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges directly related to how high the bridge is?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the higher spans are the ones that really bother me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see one coming up—on the map or out the windshield—it feels like the whole thing could drop away underneath us. I know that probably sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less if you were on a bridge but unable to see how high up you are?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be a little less if I couldn’t see the drop, yes. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels, irrational as it sounds, like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would still be present if you were on a solid, covered bridge where you couldn't see the height or the water below?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help if I couldn’t see the height or the water, yes. I don’t want to make too much of it, but the worst part for me is when I see a high bridge coming up, either on the map or through the windshield. Even though I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still be afraid if you were on a bridge but couldn't see outside, like if the windows were blocked?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less, probably, if I truly couldn’t see outside. The seeing it is a big part of it for me—seeing the high span ahead through the windshield, or even noticing it on the map beforehand. I know thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, so I’m not saying it makes sense, but once I know it’s there, it feels like the whole thing could just drop away under us. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. The sight of it is what seems to set me off. I know, rationally, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without any trouble, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you experience a similar level of fear if you were in a situation that involved heights but not a bridge, such as being near the edge of a cliff or on a high balcony?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be quite the same, no. I mean, heights aren’t my favorite, but the thing that really gets me is the bridge itself. When I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re asking about, but that’s how it seems to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I know that may sound a bit silly at my age, but when I realize I can’t avoid crossing one, my heart starts pounding right away. My hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear before I’m even on the bridge. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly bridges, yes. I don’t mean to make too much of it, but especially the high ones. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day without anything happening, but if I see one coming up on a map or through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just… waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across a bridge?\n\nPatient: It can happen either way, really. I don’t have to be the one driving. Once it’s clear we can’t avoid crossing the bridge, I start reacting before we’re even on it—my heart pounds, my hands get damp on the wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear. I know it sounds a bit much, but that’s how it comes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience fear or anxiety in other enclosed or high places, such as tunnels or tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor. At least, I haven’t noticed the same sort of thing with tunnels or tall buildings. It’s really the bridges that seem to set me off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges less intense when you cross a low bridge, such as one over a small stream?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is less intense if it’s just a low little bridge. It’s really those high spans that get to me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but when I see one coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. I realize that’s not very logical.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges directly related to how high the bridge is?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the higher spans are the ones that really bother me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see one coming up—on the map or out the windshield—it feels like the whole thing could drop away underneath us. I know that probably sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less if you were on a bridge but unable to see how high up you are?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be a little less if I couldn’t see the drop, yes. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose it’s this trouble I’ve been having with bridges. I know that may sound a bit silly at my age, but when I realize I can’t avoid crossing one, my heart starts pounding right away. My hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear before I’m even on the bridge. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly bridges, yes. I don’t mean to make too much of it, but especially the high ones. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day without anything happening, but if I see one coming up on a map or through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just… waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across a bridge?\n\nPatient: It can happen either way, really. I don’t have to be the one driving. Once it’s clear we can’t avoid crossing the bridge, I start reacting before we’re even on it—my heart pounds, my hands get damp on the wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear. I know it sounds a bit much, but that’s how it comes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience fear or anxiety in other enclosed or high places, such as tunnels or tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor. At least, I haven’t noticed the same sort of thing with tunnels or tall buildings. It’s really the bridges that seem to set me off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges less intense when you cross a low bridge, such as one over a small stream?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is less intense if it’s just a low little bridge. It’s really those high spans that get to me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but when I see one coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. I realize that’s not very logical.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges directly related to how high the bridge is?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the higher spans are the ones that really bother me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see one coming up—on the map or out the windshield—it feels like the whole thing could drop away underneath us. I know that probably sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less if you were on a bridge but unable to see how high up you are?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be a little less if I couldn’t see the drop, yes. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels, irrational as it sounds, like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would still be present if you were on a solid, covered bridge where you couldn't see the height or the water below?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help if I couldn’t see the height or the water, yes. I don’t want to make too much of it, but the worst part for me is when I see a high bridge coming up, either on the map or through the windshield. Even though I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still be afraid if you were on a bridge but couldn't see outside, like if the windows were blocked?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less, probably, if I truly couldn’t see outside. The seeing it is a big part of it for me—seeing the high span ahead through the windshield, or even noticing it on the map beforehand. I know thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, so I’m not saying it makes sense, but once I know it’s there, it feels like the whole thing could just drop away under us. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. The sight of it is what seems to set me off. I know, rationally, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without any trouble, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you experience a similar level of fear if you were in a situation that involved heights but not a bridge, such as being near the edge of a cliff or on a high balcony?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be quite the same, no. I mean, heights aren’t my favorite, but the thing that really gets me is the bridge itself. When I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re asking about, but that’s how it seems to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were in a tunnel that was as high above the ground as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not as much, if it felt like a tunnel and I wasn’t seeing the drop. It’s really when I see that high bridge span on the map or coming up through the windshield that it starts. I know perfectly well lots of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high observation deck with a glass floor, allowing you to see the ground far below?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel uneasy, yes, seeing that far down. But it still feels a little different from the bridge thing, if that makes sense. With bridges, I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s what happens in my mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose it’s this trouble I’ve been having with bridges. I know that may sound a bit silly at my age, but when I realize I can’t avoid crossing one, my heart starts pounding right away. My hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear before I’m even on the bridge. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly bridges, yes. I don’t mean to make too much of it, but especially the high ones. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day without anything happening, but if I see one coming up on a map or through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just… waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across a bridge?\n\nPatient: It can happen either way, really. I don’t have to be the one driving. Once it’s clear we can’t avoid crossing the bridge, I start reacting before we’re even on it—my heart pounds, my hands get damp on the wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear. I know it sounds a bit much, but that’s how it comes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience fear or anxiety in other enclosed or high places, such as tunnels or tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor. At least, I haven’t noticed the same sort of thing with tunnels or tall buildings. It’s really the bridges that seem to set me off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges less intense when you cross a low bridge, such as one over a small stream?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is less intense if it’s just a low little bridge. It’s really those high spans that get to me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but when I see one coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. I realize that’s not very logical.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges directly related to how high the bridge is?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the higher spans are the ones that really bother me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see one coming up—on the map or out the windshield—it feels like the whole thing could drop away underneath us. I know that probably sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less if you were on a bridge but unable to see how high up you are?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be a little less if I couldn’t see the drop, yes. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels, irrational as it sounds, like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would still be present if you were on a solid, covered bridge where you couldn't see the height or the water below?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help if I couldn’t see the height or the water, yes. I don’t want to make too much of it, but the worst part for me is when I see a high bridge coming up, either on the map or through the windshield. Even though I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still be afraid if you were on a bridge but couldn't see outside, like if the windows were blocked?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less, probably, if I truly couldn’t see outside. The seeing it is a big part of it for me—seeing the high span ahead through the windshield, or even noticing it on the map beforehand. I know thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, so I’m not saying it makes sense, but once I know it’s there, it feels like the whole thing could just drop away under us. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. The sight of it is what seems to set me off. I know, rationally, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without any trouble, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you experience a similar level of fear if you were in a situation that involved heights but not a bridge, such as being near the edge of a cliff or on a high balcony?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be quite the same, no. I mean, heights aren’t my favorite, but the thing that really gets me is the bridge itself. When I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re asking about, but that’s how it seems to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were in a tunnel that was as high above the ground as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not as much, if it felt like a tunnel and I wasn’t seeing the drop. It’s really when I see that high bridge span on the map or coming up through the windshield that it starts. I know perfectly well lots of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high observation deck with a glass floor, allowing you to see the ground far below?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel uneasy, yes, seeing that far down. But it still feels a little different from the bridge thing, if that makes sense. With bridges, I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s what happens in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a high but fully enclosed elevator?\n\nPatient: I think in a fully enclosed elevator, probably not the same, doctor. I might not love it, but the bridge is the part that really sets it off. When I see one of those high spans on the map, or coming up through the windshield, I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day… but it still feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I hope that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know that probably sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less if you were on a bridge but unable to see how high up you are?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be a little less if I couldn’t see the drop, yes. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels, irrational as it sounds, like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would still be present if you were on a solid, covered bridge where you couldn't see the height or the water below?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help if I couldn’t see the height or the water, yes. I don’t want to make too much of it, but the worst part for me is when I see a high bridge coming up, either on the map or through the windshield. Even though I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still be afraid if you were on a bridge but couldn't see outside, like if the windows were blocked?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less, probably, if I truly couldn’t see outside. The seeing it is a big part of it for me—seeing the high span ahead through the windshield, or even noticing it on the map beforehand. I know thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, so I’m not saying it makes sense, but once I know it’s there, it feels like the whole thing could just drop away under us. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. The sight of it is what seems to set me off. I know, rationally, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without any trouble, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you experience a similar level of fear if you were in a situation that involved heights but not a bridge, such as being near the edge of a cliff or on a high balcony?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be quite the same, no. I mean, heights aren’t my favorite, but the thing that really gets me is the bridge itself. When I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re asking about, but that’s how it seems to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were in a tunnel that was as high above the ground as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not as much, if it felt like a tunnel and I wasn’t seeing the drop. It’s really when I see that high bridge span on the map or coming up through the windshield that it starts. I know perfectly well lots of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high observation deck with a glass floor, allowing you to see the ground far below?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel uneasy, yes, seeing that far down. But it still feels a little different from the bridge thing, if that makes sense. With bridges, I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s what happens in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a high but fully enclosed elevator?\n\nPatient: I think in a fully enclosed elevator, probably not the same, doctor. I might not love it, but the bridge is the part that really sets it off. When I see one of those high spans on the map, or coming up through the windshield, I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day… but it still feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be the same if you were standing on a high, open platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think it would be quite the same. I mean, I know I may be splitting hairs, doctor, but the bridge is what really gets to me. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, and still, when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away under us. With just a platform… I might be nervous, but it’s not that same thought, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels, irrational as it sounds, like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would still be present if you were on a solid, covered bridge where you couldn't see the height or the water below?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help if I couldn’t see the height or the water, yes. I don’t want to make too much of it, but the worst part for me is when I see a high bridge coming up, either on the map or through the windshield. Even though I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still be afraid if you were on a bridge but couldn't see outside, like if the windows were blocked?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less, probably, if I truly couldn’t see outside. The seeing it is a big part of it for me—seeing the high span ahead through the windshield, or even noticing it on the map beforehand. I know thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, so I’m not saying it makes sense, but once I know it’s there, it feels like the whole thing could just drop away under us. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. The sight of it is what seems to set me off. I know, rationally, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without any trouble, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you experience a similar level of fear if you were in a situation that involved heights but not a bridge, such as being near the edge of a cliff or on a high balcony?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be quite the same, no. I mean, heights aren’t my favorite, but the thing that really gets me is the bridge itself. When I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re asking about, but that’s how it seems to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were in a tunnel that was as high above the ground as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not as much, if it felt like a tunnel and I wasn’t seeing the drop. It’s really when I see that high bridge span on the map or coming up through the windshield that it starts. I know perfectly well lots of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high observation deck with a glass floor, allowing you to see the ground far below?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel uneasy, yes, seeing that far down. But it still feels a little different from the bridge thing, if that makes sense. With bridges, I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s what happens in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a high but fully enclosed elevator?\n\nPatient: I think in a fully enclosed elevator, probably not the same, doctor. I might not love it, but the bridge is the part that really sets it off. When I see one of those high spans on the map, or coming up through the windshield, I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day… but it still feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be the same if you were standing on a high, open platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think it would be quite the same. I mean, I know I may be splitting hairs, doctor, but the bridge is what really gets to me. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, and still, when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away under us. With just a platform… I might be nervous, but it’s not that same thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high, enclosed catwalk or walkway that is not a bridge?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but not quite in the same way, if I’m answering that right. Being high up can bother me, sure, but the thing that really gets into my head is a bridge. I know perfectly well all those cars go over them every day, and they’re built for it, but when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—it feels like it’s just waiting to drop away underneath us. A catwalk might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t quite have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were on a high, enclosed observation deck over water, but not on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, doctor, just because it’s high and over water, but I don’t think it would be the same. My hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear before I’m even on the bridge. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly bridges, yes. I don’t mean to make too much of it, but especially the high ones. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day without anything happening, but if I see one coming up on a map or through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just… waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across a bridge?\n\nPatient: It can happen either way, really. I don’t have to be the one driving. Once it’s clear we can’t avoid crossing the bridge, I start reacting before we’re even on it—my heart pounds, my hands get damp on the wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear. I know it sounds a bit much, but that’s how it comes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience fear or anxiety in other enclosed or high places, such as tunnels or tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor. At least, I haven’t noticed the same sort of thing with tunnels or tall buildings. It’s really the bridges that seem to set me off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges less intense when you cross a low bridge, such as one over a small stream?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is less intense if it’s just a low little bridge. It’s really those high spans that get to me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but when I see one coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. I realize that’s not very logical.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges directly related to how high the bridge is?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the higher spans are the ones that really bother me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see one coming up—on the map or out the windshield—it feels like the whole thing could drop away underneath us. I know that probably sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less if you were on a bridge but unable to see how high up you are?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be a little less if I couldn’t see the drop, yes. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels, irrational as it sounds, like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would still be present if you were on a solid, covered bridge where you couldn't see the height or the water below?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help if I couldn’t see the height or the water, yes. I don’t want to make too much of it, but the worst part for me is when I see a high bridge coming up, either on the map or through the windshield. Even though I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still be afraid if you were on a bridge but couldn't see outside, like if the windows were blocked?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less, probably, if I truly couldn’t see outside. The seeing it is a big part of it for me—seeing the high span ahead through the windshield, or even noticing it on the map beforehand. I know thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, so I’m not saying it makes sense, but once I know it’s there, it feels like the whole thing could just drop away under us. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. The sight of it is what seems to set me off. I know, rationally, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without any trouble, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you experience a similar level of fear if you were in a situation that involved heights but not a bridge, such as being near the edge of a cliff or on a high balcony?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be quite the same, no. I mean, heights aren’t my favorite, but the thing that really gets me is the bridge itself. When I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re asking about, but that’s how it seems to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were in a tunnel that was as high above the ground as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not as much, if it felt like a tunnel and I wasn’t seeing the drop. It’s really when I see that high bridge span on the map or coming up through the windshield that it starts. I know perfectly well lots of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high observation deck with a glass floor, allowing you to see the ground far below?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel uneasy, yes, seeing that far down. But it still feels a little different from the bridge thing, if that makes sense. With bridges, I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s what happens in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a high but fully enclosed elevator?\n\nPatient: I think in a fully enclosed elevator, probably not the same, doctor. I might not love it, but the bridge is the part that really sets it off. When I see one of those high spans on the map, or coming up through the windshield, I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day… but it still feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be the same if you were standing on a high, open platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think it would be quite the same. I mean, I know I may be splitting hairs, doctor, but the bridge is what really gets to me. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, and still, when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away under us. With just a platform… I might be nervous, but it’s not that same thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high, enclosed catwalk or walkway that is not a bridge?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but not quite in the same way, if I’m answering that right. Being high up can bother me, sure, but the thing that really gets into my head is a bridge. I know perfectly well all those cars go over them every day, and they’re built for it, but when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—it feels like it’s just waiting to drop away underneath us. A catwalk might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t quite have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were on a high, enclosed observation deck over water, but not on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, doctor, just because it’s high and over water, but I don’t think it would be the same. I don’t want to overstate it. The thing that really sets me off is a bridge—when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over those every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be the same if you were in a similar high and open situation, but it wasn't a bridge, such as standing on a high cliff edge?\n\nPatient: Not exactly the same, no. A cliff edge would probably make me uneasy, I suppose, but the bridge is the thing that really sets it off for me. I know it sounds a bit unreasonable, and I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but when I see a high span on the map or up ahead through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. That’s the part I mean, if I’m explaining it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were on a similar elevated structure, such as a high pier or a dock, that was not a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe some, yes, if it were high up… but I don’t think it would be quite the same. I don’t mean to make too much of it. The thing I keep coming back to is bridges. I know perfectly well that thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up ahead through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. A pier or dock might make me uncomfortable, but that bridge feeling is different, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

A pier or dock might make me uncomfortable, but that bridge feeling is different, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould your fear be present if you were on a high, but very sturdy and wide, overpass that is not a bridge, but still has a similar height and exposure to the ground below?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 87 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but when I see a high span on the map or ahead through the windshield, it feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away under us.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Some, maybe, because of the height… but if it’s really wide and sturdy and not a bridge, I don’t think it would hit me quite the same way. I know I keep circling back to this, but the real fear is when it’s a bridge—when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but in that moment it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I hope that’s a clear distinction.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose it’s this trouble I’ve been having with bridges. I know that may sound a bit silly at my age, but when I realize I can’t avoid crossing one, my heart starts pounding right away. My hands get damp on the steering wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear before I’m even on the bridge. I’m not sure if that’s the sort of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations or places that cause a similar reaction?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly bridges, yes. I don’t mean to make too much of it, but especially the high ones. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day without anything happening, but if I see one coming up on a map or through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is just… waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bridges occur only when you're driving across them, or does it also happen when you're walking or being driven across a bridge?\n\nPatient: It can happen either way, really. I don’t have to be the one driving. Once it’s clear we can’t avoid crossing the bridge, I start reacting before we’re even on it—my heart pounds, my hands get damp on the wheel or the armrest, and I get this awful rush of fear. I know it sounds a bit much, but that’s how it comes on.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience fear or anxiety in other enclosed or high places, such as tunnels or tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor. At least, I haven’t noticed the same sort of thing with tunnels or tall buildings. It’s really the bridges that seem to set me off. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges less intense when you cross a low bridge, such as one over a small stream?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is less intense if it’s just a low little bridge. It’s really those high spans that get to me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day, but when I see one coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. I realize that’s not very logical.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges directly related to how high the bridge is?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the higher spans are the ones that really bother me. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see one coming up—on the map or out the windshield—it feels like the whole thing could drop away underneath us. I know that probably sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less if you were on a bridge but unable to see how high up you are?\n\nPatient: I suppose it might be a little less if I couldn’t see the drop, yes. It’s when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—that my mind sort of grabs onto it. I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels, irrational as it sounds, like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would still be present if you were on a solid, covered bridge where you couldn't see the height or the water below?\n\nPatient: I think it would probably help if I couldn’t see the height or the water, yes. I don’t want to make too much of it, but the worst part for me is when I see a high bridge coming up, either on the map or through the windshield. Even though I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away underneath us. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you still be afraid if you were on a bridge but couldn't see outside, like if the windows were blocked?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less, probably, if I truly couldn’t see outside. The seeing it is a big part of it for me—seeing the high span ahead through the windshield, or even noticing it on the map beforehand. I know thousands of cars go over those bridges every day, so I’m not saying it makes sense, but once I know it’s there, it feels like the whole thing could just drop away under us. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be significantly reduced if you were crossing a high bridge but couldn't see the height or the ground below?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced some—maybe quite a bit—if I couldn’t see the height or the ground below. The sight of it is what seems to set me off. I know, rationally, that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without any trouble, but when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole bridge is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that sounds unreasonable, but that’s how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you experience a similar level of fear if you were in a situation that involved heights but not a bridge, such as being near the edge of a cliff or on a high balcony?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would be quite the same, no. I mean, heights aren’t my favorite, but the thing that really gets me is the bridge itself. When I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if that’s the distinction you’re asking about, but that’s how it seems to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were in a tunnel that was as high above the ground as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not as much, if it felt like a tunnel and I wasn’t seeing the drop. It’s really when I see that high bridge span on the map or coming up through the windshield that it starts. I know perfectly well lots of cars cross those bridges every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high observation deck with a glass floor, allowing you to see the ground far below?\n\nPatient: I suppose I might feel uneasy, yes, seeing that far down. But it still feels a little different from the bridge thing, if that makes sense. With bridges, I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over them every day, but when I see a high span coming up on the map or through the windshield, I get this feeling like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but that’s what happens in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel a similar level of fear if you were on a high but fully enclosed elevator?\n\nPatient: I think in a fully enclosed elevator, probably not the same, doctor. I might not love it, but the bridge is the part that really sets it off. When I see one of those high spans on the map, or coming up through the windshield, I know perfectly well thousands of cars cross them every day… but it still feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be the same if you were standing on a high, open platform that wasn't a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think it would be quite the same. I mean, I know I may be splitting hairs, doctor, but the bridge is what really gets to me. I can know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day, and still, when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield, it feels like the whole thing is waiting to drop away under us. With just a platform… I might be nervous, but it’s not that same thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a high, enclosed catwalk or walkway that is not a bridge?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but not quite in the same way, if I’m answering that right. Being high up can bother me, sure, but the thing that really gets into my head is a bridge. I know perfectly well all those cars go over them every day, and they’re built for it, but when I see a high span coming up—on the map or through the windshield—it feels like it’s just waiting to drop away underneath us. A catwalk might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t quite have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be present if you were on a high, enclosed observation deck over water, but not on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, doctor, just because it’s high and over water, but I don’t think it would be the same. I don’t want to overstate it. The thing that really sets me off is a bridge—when I see a high span on the map or coming up through the windshield. I know perfectly well thousands of cars go over those every day, but it feels to me like the whole thing is just waiting to drop away under us. Does that answer it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear be the same if you were in a similar high and open situation, but it wasn't a bridge, such as standing on a high cliff edge?\n\nPatient: Not exactly the same, no.
